.

4.2
Even weight modular forms and inhomogeneous finite modular group

If the modular weight
k
is an even positive integer, Eq. (
114
) gives us

\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(R)=(-1)^{k}=1\,.

(116)

Thus the homogeneous finite modular group
\Gamma^{\prime}_{N}
reduces to inhomogeneous finite modular group
\Gamma_{N}
, and the even weight modular forms can be arranged into multiplets of
\Gamma_{N}
up to the automorphy factor
(c\tau+d)^{k}
. Remarkably for
N\leq 5
, the inhomogeneous finite modular groups are isomorphic to permutation groups:
\Gamma_{2}\cong S_{3}
,
\Gamma_{3}\cong A_{4}
,
\Gamma_{4}\cong S_{4}
and
\Gamma_{5}\cong A_{5}
, as shown in table
2
.

There are no modular forms of negative weights, and the weight zero modular form is a constant. Modular forms of weight
2k
and level
N

form a linear space
{\cal M}_{2k}(\Gamma(N))
of finite dimension, and the dimensions
\dim\mathcal{M}_{2k}(\Gamma(N))
for lower levels
N
are shown in table
4
. The product of two modular forms of level
N
and weights
2k
,
2k^{\prime}
is a modular form of level
N
and weight
2(k+k^{\prime})
and the set
{\cal M}(\Gamma(N))
of all modular forms of level
N
is a ring

{\cal M}(\Gamma(N))=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}{\cal M}_{2k}(\Gamma(N))~~~,

(117)

generated by few elements. For instance
{\cal M}(\Gamma)
is generated by two modular forms
E_{4}(\tau)
and
E_{6}(\tau)
of weight 4 and 6 respectively,
so that each modular form in
{\cal M}_{2k}(\Gamma)
can be written as a polynomial
\sum_{ij}c_{ij}~E_{4}(\tau)^{n_{i}}E_{6}(\tau)^{n_{j}}
, with powers satisfying
2k=4n_{i}+6n_{j}
. Hence one only needs to know the lowest weight two modular form to construct the even weight modular forms of level
N
.

4.2.1
Constructing even weight modular forms of level 3

As an example of the general procedure, we now explicitly construct the even weight modular forms of level 3.
The Dedekind eta-function
\eta(\tau)
is frequently used to construct modular forms, it was introduced by Dedekind in 1877 and it is defined as follow
diamond2005first
;
Bruinier2008The
;
lang2012introduction
,

\eta(\tau)=q^{1/24}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(1-q^{n}\right),\qquad q\equiv e^{i2\pi\tau}\,.

(118)

The
\eta(\tau)
function can also be written into the following infinite series,

\eta(\tau)=q^{1/24}\sum^{+\infty}_{n=-\infty}(-1)^{n}q^{n(3n-1)/2}\,.

(119)

Under the
S
and
T
transformations,
\eta(\tau)
behaves as
diamond2005first
;
Bruinier2008The
;
lang2012introduction

\eta(\tau+1)=e^{i\pi/12}\eta(\tau),\qquad\eta(-1/\tau)=\sqrt{-i\tau}~\eta(\tau)\,.

(120)

Hence
\eta^{24}(\tau)
is a modular form of weight 12.

In this section, we follow the method of Ref.
Feruglio:2017spp
to explicitly construct a basis of weight 2 for
\Gamma(3)
. From table
4
we know the dimension
\dim\mathcal{M}_{2}(\Gamma(3))=3
, in the following we find out three linearly independent modular forms of weight 2 and level 3. We start by observing that if
f(\tau)
transforms as
13
13

13

Notice that here
f(\tau)
is not a modular form of weight
k
because of the presence of the constant factor
e^{i\alpha}
.

f(\tau)\to e^{i\alpha}~(c\tau+d)^{k}~f(\tau)\,,

(121)

then

\frac{d}{d\tau}\log f(\tau)\to(c\tau+d)^{2}\frac{d}{d\tau}\log f(\tau)+kc\,(c\tau+d)\,.

(122)

The deviation of this formula is given in Appendix
A
. Furthermore, we can linearly combine several
f_{i}(\tau)
with weights
k_{i}
as follow

\frac{d}{d\tau}\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\log f_{i}(\tau)\to(c\tau+d)^{2}\frac{d}{d\tau}\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\log f_{i}(\tau)+\left(\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}k_{i}\right)c(c\tau+d)~~~,

(123)

with

\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}k_{i}=0\,.

(124)

Then the inhomogeneous term can be removed and consequently weight 2 modular forms can be obtained. The seed functions for the modular forms of weight 2 and level 3 are given by
Feruglio:2017spp

\displaystyle f_{1}(\tau)=\eta\left(\frac{\tau}{3}\right),~~~~f_{2}(\tau)=\eta\left(\frac{\tau+1}{3}\right)

\displaystyle f_{3}(\tau)=\eta\left(\frac{\tau+2}{3}\right),~~~~f_{4}(\tau)=\eta(3\tau)\,.

(125)

They are closed under the modular group, and each of them is mapped to itself under the action of
\Gamma(3)
. Moreover, under
T
they transform as

\displaystyle\hskip-7.22743ptf_{1}(\tau)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle T}}{{\longmapsto}}f_{2}(\tau)\,,~~f_{2}(\tau)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle T}}{{\longmapsto}}f_{3}(\tau)\,,~~f_{3}(\tau)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle T}}{{\longmapsto}}e^{i\pi/12}f_{1}(\tau),~~f_{4}(\tau)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle T}}{{\longmapsto}}e^{i\pi/4}f_{4}(\tau)\,.

(126)

From Eq. (
120
), we known that they transform under
S
in the following way,

\displaystyle f_{1}(\tau)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S}}{{\longmapsto}}\sqrt{3}~\sqrt{-i\tau}\,f_{4}(\tau)\,,~~~f_{2}(\tau)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S}}{{\longmapsto}}e^{-i\pi/12}~\sqrt{-i\tau}~f_{3}(\tau)\,,

\displaystyle f_{3}(\tau)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S}}{{\longmapsto}}e^{i\pi/12}~\sqrt{-i\tau}~f_{2}(\tau)\,,~~~f_{4}(\tau)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S}}{{\longmapsto}}\sqrt{1/3}~\sqrt{-i\tau}~f_{1}(\tau)

(127)

Hence the most general form of weight 2 and level 3 modular forms can be written as

\mathcal{F}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4}|\tau)=\frac{d}{d\tau}\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\log f_{i}(\tau)

(128)

with
\sum^{4}_{i=1}\alpha_{i}=0
to eliminate the inhomogeneous term. Under the action of modular generators
S
and
T
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4}|\tau)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S}}{{\longmapsto}}\tau^{2}~\mathcal{F}(\alpha_{4},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{1}|\tau)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4}|\tau)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle T}}{{\longmapsto}}\mathcal{F}(\alpha_{3},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{4}|\tau)\,.

(129)

As shown in Eq. (
112
), it is always possible to choose a basis in the linear space of modular form such that the independent basis vectors form irreducible multiplets of the finite modular group up to the automorphy factor. For the present case, there are three independent modular forms
Y_{i}(\tau)
which can be arranged into a
A_{4}
triplet
Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau)=(Y_{1},Y_{2},Y_{3})^{T}
satisfying

Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(-1/\tau)=\tau^{2}~\rho_{\mathbf{3}}(S)Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau)\,,~~~~Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau+1)=\rho_{\mathbf{3}}(T)Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau)\,,

(130)

where
\rho_{\mathbf{3}}(S)
and
\rho_{\mathbf{3}}(T)
are the unitary representation matrices of
S
and
T
in the triplet representation of
A_{4}
given in Appendix
C.2
,

\rho_{\mathbf{3}}(S)=\frac{1}{3}\begin{pmatrix}-1~&~2~&~2\\
2~&~-1~&~2\\
2~&~2~&~-1\end{pmatrix}\,,~~~\rho_{\mathbf{3}}(T)=\begin{pmatrix}1~&~0~&~0\\
0~&~\omega~&~0\\
0~&~0~&~\omega^{2}\end{pmatrix}\,,~~~~\omega=e^{2\pi i/3}\,.

(131)

Solving Eq. (
130
), we can obtain the explicit form of modular forms
Y_{i}(\tau)
as follows

\displaystyle Y_{1}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3i}{2\pi}~\mathcal{F}(1,1,1,-3|\tau)=\frac{i}{2\pi}\left[\frac{\eta^{\prime}\left(\frac{\tau}{3}\right)}{\eta\left(\frac{\tau}{3}\right)}+\frac{\eta^{\prime}\left(\frac{\tau+1}{3}\right)}{\eta\left(\frac{\tau+1}{3}\right)}+\frac{\eta^{\prime}\left(\frac{\tau+2}{3}\right)}{\eta\left(\frac{\tau+2}{3}\right)}-\frac{27\eta^{\prime}(3\tau)}{\eta(3\tau)}\right]\,,

\displaystyle Y_{2}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{3i}{\pi}~\mathcal{F}(1,\omega^{2},\omega,0|\tau)=\frac{-i}{\pi}\left[\frac{\eta^{\prime}\left(\frac{\tau}{3}\right)}{\eta\left(\frac{\tau}{3}\right)}+\omega^{2}~\frac{\eta^{\prime}\left(\frac{\tau+1}{3}\right)}{\eta\left(\frac{\tau+1}{3}\right)}+\omega~\frac{\eta^{\prime}\left(\frac{\tau+2}{3}\right)}{\eta\left(\frac{\tau+2}{3}\right)}\right]\,,

\displaystyle Y_{3}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{3i}{\pi}~\mathcal{F}(1,\omega,\omega^{2},0|\tau)=\frac{-i}{\pi}\left[\frac{\eta^{\prime}\left(\frac{\tau}{3}\right)}{\eta\left(\frac{\tau}{3}\right)}+\omega~\frac{\eta^{\prime}\left(\frac{\tau+1}{3}\right)}{\eta\left(\frac{\tau+1}{3}\right)}+\omega^{2}~\frac{\eta^{\prime}\left(\frac{\tau+2}{3}\right)}{\eta\left(\frac{\tau+2}{3}\right)}\right]\,,

(132)

up to an overall constant which can be chosen freely. In numerical calculation, one usually needs
q
-expansion of
Y_{i}
,

\displaystyle Y_{1}(\tau)=1+12q+36q^{2}+12q^{3}+84q^{4}+72q^{5}+36q^{6}+96q^{7}+180q^{8}+12q^{9}+216q^{10}+\dots\,,

\displaystyle Y_{2}(\tau)=-6q^{1/3}\left(1+7q+8q^{2}+18q^{3}+14q^{4}+31q^{5}+20q^{6}+36q^{7}+31q^{8}+56q^{9}+32q^{10}+\dots\right)\,,

\displaystyle Y_{3}(\tau)=-18q^{2/3}\left(1+2q+5q^{2}+4q^{3}+8q^{4}+6q^{5}+14q^{6}+8q^{7}+14q^{8}+10q^{9}+21q^{10}+\dots\right)~~\,.

(133)

From the above
q
-expansion we see that the modular forms
Y_{i}(\tau)
satisfy the constraint:

Y_{2}^{2}+2Y_{1}Y_{3}=0\,.

(134)

The whole ring of even weight modular forms of level 3 can be generated from the products of the weight 2 modular forms
Y_{1,2,3}(\tau)
. At weight 4, the tensor product of
Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}
gives rise to three independent modular multiplets,

\displaystyle Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}})_{\mathbf{1}}=Y_{1}^{2}+2Y_{2}Y_{3}\,,

\displaystyle Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}})_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime}}=Y_{3}^{2}+2Y_{1}Y_{2}\,,

\displaystyle Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}})_{\mathbf{3}_{S}}=\begin{pmatrix}Y_{1}^{2}-Y_{2}Y_{3}\\
Y_{3}^{2}-Y_{1}Y_{2}\\
Y_{2}^{2}-Y_{1}Y_{3}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(135)

There are seven linearly independent modular forms of level 3 and weight 6 and they decompose as
\mathbf{1}\oplus\mathbf{3}\oplus\mathbf{3}
under
A_{4}
,

\displaystyle Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}})_{\mathbf{1}}=Y_{1}^{3}+Y_{2}^{3}+Y_{3}^{3}-3Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3}\,,

\displaystyle Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}I}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}=(Y_{1}^{2}+2Y_{2}Y_{3})\begin{pmatrix}Y_{1}\\
Y_{2}\\
Y_{3}\end{pmatrix}\,,

\displaystyle Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}II}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime}}=(Y_{3}^{2}+2Y_{1}Y_{2})\begin{pmatrix}Y_{3}\\
Y_{1}\\
Y_{2}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(136)

The weight 8 modular forms can be arranged into three singlets
\mathbf{1}
,
\mathbf{1}^{\prime}
,
\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}
and two triplets
\mathbf{3}
of
A_{4}
,

\displaystyle Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}I})_{\mathbf{1}}=(Y_{1}^{2}+2Y_{2}Y_{3})^{2}\,,

\displaystyle Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}I})_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}=(Y_{1}^{2}+2Y_{2}Y_{3})(Y_{3}^{2}+2Y_{1}Y_{2})\,,

\displaystyle Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}II})_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}=(Y_{3}^{2}+2Y_{1}Y_{2})^{2}\,,

\displaystyle Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{3}I}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{1}}=(Y_{1}^{3}+Y_{2}^{3}+Y_{3}^{3}-3Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3})\begin{pmatrix}Y_{1}\\
Y_{2}\\
Y_{3}\end{pmatrix}\,,

\displaystyle Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{3}II}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}II})_{\mathbf{3}_{A}}=(Y_{3}^{2}+2Y_{1}Y_{2})\begin{pmatrix}Y^{2}_{2}-Y_{1}Y_{3}\\
Y^{2}_{1}-Y_{2}Y_{3}\\
Y^{2}_{3}-Y_{1}Y_{2}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(137)

The weight 10 modular forms of level 3 decompose as
\mathbf{1}\oplus\mathbf{1}^{\prime}\oplus\mathbf{3}\oplus\mathbf{3}\oplus\mathbf{3}
under
A_{4}
, and they are

\displaystyle Y^{(10)}_{\mathbf{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{3}I})_{\mathbf{1}}=(Y_{1}^{2}+2Y_{2}Y_{3})(Y_{1}^{3}+Y_{2}^{3}+Y_{3}^{3}-3Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3})\,,

\displaystyle Y^{(10)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{3}I})_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}=(Y_{3}^{2}+2Y_{1}Y_{2})(Y_{1}^{3}+Y_{2}^{3}+Y_{3}^{3}-3Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3})\,,

\displaystyle Y^{(10)}_{\mathbf{3}I}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1}}=(Y_{1}^{2}+2Y_{2}Y_{3})^{2}\begin{pmatrix}Y_{1}\\
Y_{2}\\
Y_{3}\end{pmatrix}\,,

\displaystyle Y^{(10)}_{\mathbf{3}II}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime}}=(Y_{1}^{2}+2Y_{2}Y_{3})(Y_{3}^{2}+2Y_{1}Y_{2})\begin{pmatrix}Y_{3}\\
Y_{1}\\
Y_{2}\end{pmatrix}\,,

\displaystyle Y^{(10)}_{\mathbf{3}III}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}}=(Y_{3}^{2}+2Y_{1}Y_{2})^{2}\begin{pmatrix}Y_{2}\\
Y_{3}\\
Y_{1}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(138)

Higher weight modular forms can be constructed analogously, and one should consider the nonlinear constraints such as Eq. (
134
) to pick out the linearly independent modular forms from all possible tensor products. It is straightforward although tedious.

In the same fashion, the weight two modular forms of level 2 and level 4 can be constructed. There are two independent modular forms of weight 2 at level 2
Kobayashi:2018vbk
,

\displaystyle\frac{i}{4\pi}\left[\frac{\eta^{\prime}\left(\frac{\tau}{2}\right)}{\eta\left(\frac{\tau}{2}\right)}+\frac{\eta^{\prime}\left(\frac{\tau+1}{2}\right)}{\eta\left(\frac{\tau+1}{2}\right)}-\frac{8\eta^{\prime}(2\tau)}{\eta(2\tau)}\right]\,,~~~~~~~\frac{\sqrt{3}i}{4\pi}\left[\frac{\eta^{\prime}\left(\frac{\tau}{2}\right)}{\eta\left(\frac{\tau}{2}\right)}-\frac{\eta^{\prime}\left(\frac{\tau+1}{2}\right)}{\eta\left(\frac{\tau+1}{2}\right)}\right]\,,

(139)

which form a
S_{3}
doublet. The basis of the weight 2 modular forms at level 4 can be chosen as
Penedo:2018nmg
;
Novichkov:2018ovf

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{8\pi}\left[\frac{\eta^{\prime}\left(\frac{\tau}{4}\right)}{\eta\left(\frac{\tau}{4}\right)}+\frac{\eta^{\prime}\left(\frac{\tau+1}{4}\right)}{\eta\left(\frac{\tau+1}{4}\right)}+\frac{\eta^{\prime}\left(\frac{\tau+2}{4}\right)}{\eta\left(\frac{\tau+2}{4}\right)}+\frac{\eta^{\prime}\left(\frac{\tau+3}{4}\right)}{\eta\left(\frac{\tau+3}{4}\right)}-\frac{8\eta^{\prime}\left(\tau+\frac{1}{2}\right)}{\eta\left(\tau+\frac{1}{2}\right)}-\frac{32\eta^{\prime}(4\tau)}{\eta(4\tau)}\right]\,,

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{3}\,i}{8\pi}\left[\frac{\eta^{\prime}\left(\frac{\tau}{4}\right)}{\eta\left(\frac{\tau}{4}\right)}-\frac{\eta^{\prime}\left(\frac{\tau+1}{4}\right)}{\eta\left(\frac{\tau+1}{4}\right)}+\frac{\eta^{\prime}\left(\frac{\tau+2}{4}\right)}{\eta\left(\frac{\tau+2}{4}\right)}-\frac{\eta^{\prime}\left(\frac{\tau+3}{4}\right)}{\eta\left(\frac{\tau+3}{4}\right)}\right]\,,

\displaystyle\frac{i}{\pi}\left[\frac{\eta^{\prime}\left(\tau+\frac{1}{2}\right)}{\eta\left(\tau+\frac{1}{2}\right)}-\frac{4\eta^{\prime}(4\tau)}{\eta(4\tau)}\right]\,,

\displaystyle\frac{-\sqrt{2}\,i}{8\pi}\left[\frac{\eta^{\prime}\left(\frac{\tau}{4}\right)}{\eta\left(\frac{\tau}{4}\right)}-i\frac{\eta^{\prime}\left(\frac{\tau+1}{4}\right)}{\eta\left(\frac{\tau+1}{4}\right)}-\frac{\eta^{\prime}\left(\frac{\tau+2}{4}\right)}{\eta\left(\frac{\tau+2}{4}\right)}+i\frac{\eta^{\prime}\left(\frac{\tau+3}{4}\right)}{\eta\left(\frac{\tau+3}{4}\right)}\right]\,,

\displaystyle\frac{-\sqrt{2}\,i}{8\pi}\left[\frac{\eta^{\prime}\left(\frac{\tau}{4}\right)}{\eta\left(\frac{\tau}{4}\right)}+i\frac{\eta^{\prime}\left(\frac{\tau+1}{4}\right)}{\eta\left(\frac{\tau+1}{4}\right)}-\frac{\eta^{\prime}\left(\frac{\tau+2}{4}\right)}{\eta\left(\frac{\tau+2}{4}\right)}-i\frac{\eta^{\prime}\left(\frac{\tau+3}{4}\right)}{\eta\left(\frac{\tau+3}{4}\right)}\right]\,,

(140)

which can be organized into a doublet and a triplet of
S_{4}

Penedo:2018nmg
;
Novichkov:2018ovf
. For
N\geq 5
, the linear combinations of the logarithmic derivatives of
\eta
function is not enough generate the whole modular form space, and consequently other seed functions suh as Klein forms and Jacobi constants are necessary. See Refs.
Novichkov:2018nkm
;
Ding:2019xna
and
Ding:2020msi
for the construction of modular forms of level 5 and level 7 respectively. We summarize the even weight modular forms and their decompositions under
\Gamma_{N}
in table
4
for
N\leq 7
.

Integer weight modular forms of level
N
and the decomposition under the homogeneous finite modular group
\Gamma^{\prime}_{N}
. Notice that odd weight modular form doesn’t exist for
\Gamma(2)
. The conventions for the irreducible representations follow
Liu:2019khw
for
\Gamma^{\prime}_{3}
,

As shown in the beginning of section
4
, the principal congruence subgroups
\Gamma(N)
for
N>2
have odd weight modular forms because of
S^{2}\notin\Gamma(N>2)
, and the general integer weight modular forms of level
N
can be arranged into irreducible multiplets of the homogeneous finite modular group
\Gamma^{\prime}_{N}
which is the double cover of
\Gamma_{N}
. The groups
\Gamma^{\prime}_{N}
for lower levels
N
are summarized in in table
2
, they could be realized in orbifold compactifications on
T^{2}\times T^{2}
with magnetic fluxes
Kikuchi:2020nxn
. Notice that the odd weight and even weight modular forms are in the representations
\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(R)=-1
and
\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(R)=1
respectively. All the modular forms can be obtained from the products of the lowest weight 1 modular forms and they can be constructed from the Dedekind
\eta
function and Klein forms for small levels
N\leq 6
. In the following, we present the linearly independent basis for the linear space
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\Gamma(N))
of weight 1 modular forms
schultz2015notes
;
Li:2021buv
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{1}(\Gamma(3))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\frac{\eta^{3}(3\tau)}{\eta(\tau)},~\frac{\eta^{3}(\tau/3)}{\eta(\tau)}\right\}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{1}(\Gamma(4))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\frac{\eta^{4}(4\tau)}{\eta^{2}(2\tau)},~\frac{\eta^{10}(2\tau)}{\eta^{4}(4\tau)\eta^{4}(\tau)},~\frac{\eta^{4}(2\tau)}{\eta^{2}(2\tau)}\right\}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{1}(\Gamma(5))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big\{\frac{\eta^{15}(5\tau)}{\eta^{3}(\tau)}\mathfrak{k}^{5}_{\frac{2}{5},0}(5\tau),~\frac{\eta^{15}(5\tau)}{\eta^{3}(\tau)}\mathfrak{k}_{\frac{1}{5},0}(5\tau)\mathfrak{k}^{4}_{\frac{2}{5},0}(5\tau),~\frac{\eta^{15}(5\tau)}{\eta^{3}(\tau)}\mathfrak{k}^{2}_{\frac{1}{5},0}(5\tau)\mathfrak{k}^{3}_{\frac{2}{5},0}(5\tau),

\displaystyle~\frac{\eta^{15}(5\tau)}{\eta^{3}(\tau)}\mathfrak{k}^{3}_{\frac{1}{5},0}(5\tau)\mathfrak{k}^{2}_{\frac{2}{5},0}(5\tau),~\frac{\eta^{15}(5\tau)}{\eta^{3}(\tau)}\mathfrak{k}^{4}_{\frac{1}{5},0}(5\tau)\mathfrak{k}_{\frac{2}{5},0}(5\tau),~\frac{\eta^{15}(5\tau)}{\eta^{3}(\tau)}\mathfrak{k}^{5}_{\frac{1}{5},0}(5\tau)\,.\Big\}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{1}(\Gamma(6))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\frac{\eta^{3}(3\tau)}{\eta(\tau)},~\frac{\eta^{3}(\tau/3)}{\eta(\tau)},~\frac{\eta^{3}(6\tau)}{\eta(2\tau)},~\frac{\eta^{3}(\tau/6)}{\eta(\tau/2)},~\frac{\eta^{3}(2\tau/3)}{\eta(2\tau)},~\frac{\eta^{3}(3\tau/2)}{\eta(\tau/2)}\right\}\,,

(141)

where the Klein form
\mathfrak{k}_{(r_{1},r_{2})}(\tau)
is a holomorphic function which has no zeros and poles on the upper half complex plane. The Klein form can be written into an infinite product expansion
K_lang1981
;
lang1987elliptic
;
lang2012introduction
;
eum2011modularity
:

\mathfrak{k}_{(r_{1},r_{2})}(\tau)=q^{(r_{1}-1)/2}_{z}(1-q_{z})\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1-q^{n}q_{z})(1-q^{n}q_{z}^{-1})(1-q^{n})^{-2}\,,

(142)

with
q_{z}=e^{2\pi iz}
with
z=r_{1}\tau+r_{2}
. The integer weight modular forms up to weight 3 and the decomposition under
\Gamma^{\prime}_{N}
are listed in table
5
.

4.4
Half-integer weight modular form and metaplectic cover

Let us first consider the problem for half-integer weight modular forms for the purposes of illustration. Then later we provide the solution for half-integer weight modular forms using the metaplectic cover.

Similar to integer weight modular form, one may attempt to define the half-integer weight modular form as a holomorphic function of
\tau
and satisfying

Y(\gamma\tau)=(c\tau+d)^{k/2}Y(\tau)=J^{k/2}(\gamma,\tau)Y(\tau),~~~~\gamma=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in SL(2,\mathbb{Z})

(143)

for a positive integer
k
. We see that the square root of the
c\tau+d
appears in the above transformation formula. It is known that the function of the square root has two branches
\sqrt{z}=\left\{z^{1/2},-z^{1/2}\right\}
for a complex number
z
. It is crucial to deal with the two branches for the square root in a systematic way.
The most common choice and the one we will always use is to choose the principal branch of the square root, i.e.,

z^{1/2}\equiv|z|^{1/2}\exp\left(i\frac{\texttt{Arg}(z)}{2}\right),~~~-\pi<\texttt{Arg}(z)\leq\pi\,.

(144)

Then
z^{1/2}
for real
z<0
is a pure positive imaginary number and
(-1)^{1/2}=i
. Hence
(z_{1}z_{2})^{1/2}
is equal to
z_{1}^{1/2}z_{2}^{1/2}
only up to a sign
\pm 1
, i.e.,

\displaystyle(z_{1}z_{2})^{1/2}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}z_{1}^{1/2}z_{2}^{1/2},&~-\pi<\texttt{Arg}(z_{1})+\texttt{Arg}(z_{2})\leq\pi\\
-z_{1}^{1/2}z_{2}^{1/2},&~\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

For an (even or odd) integer
k
,
z^{k/2}
always refer to
(z^{1/2})^{k}
. Note that this is not always equal to
(z^{k})^{1/2}
for
k
odd. Because of the multivalues of the square root function,
J^{k/2}(\gamma,\tau)=(c\tau+d)^{k/2}
doesn’t satisfy the cocycle relation
stromberg2013weil
;
cohen2017modular

J^{k/2}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\tau)J^{k/2}(\gamma_{2},\tau)=\zeta^{k}_{1/2}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})J^{k/2}(\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2},\tau)\neq J^{k/2}(\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2},\tau),\quad\gamma_{1,2}\in\Gamma\,,

(148)

where the two-cocycle
\zeta_{1/2}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})
can only take values
+1
and
-1
, and its explicit expression is given in
Liu:2020msy
. Notice that
\zeta^{k}_{1/2}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})
is always equal to 1 for any values of
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
if
k
is even. As a consequence, and the definition in Eq. (
143
) is inconsistent, i.e.

Y(\gamma_{1}(\gamma_{2}(\tau)))\neq Y(\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}(\tau))\,.

(149)

Hence
(c\tau+d)^{k/2}
is not the automorphy factor anymore. It turns out that the half-integral weight modular forms can only be defined on the principal congruence subgroup
\Gamma(4N)
, and a multiplier is generally needed. The weight
k/2
weight modular form
Y(\tau)
with
k
positive integer is a holomorphic function of
\tau
and satisfies the following condition
shimura1973modular
:

Y(h\tau)=v^{k}(h)(c\tau+d)^{k/2}Y(\tau)=v^{k}(h)J^{k/2}(h,\tau)Y(\tau),\qquad h=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma(4N)\,,

(150)

where
v(h)=(\frac{c}{d})
is the Kronecker symbol, it is
1
or
-1
here and its more detailed properties can be found in
Liu:2020msy
. The Kronecker symbol
v(h)
fulfills the following identity
stromberg2013weil
;
cohen2017modular
:

v(h_{1}h_{2})=\zeta_{1/2}(h_{1},h_{2})v(h_{1})v(h_{2}),~~~h_{1,2}\in\Gamma(4N)\,.

(151)

Thus one can check that
v^{k}(h)(c\tau+d)^{k/2}
is the correct automorphy factor and it satisfies the cocycle relation, and the ambiguity caused by half-integral weight is eliminated by the factor
v(h)
.

Now we provide the solution to the problem of modular forms for half-integer modular weights using metaplectic cover.
In order to consistently define the half-integer modular forms, one should consider both branches of the square root function and consider the metaplectic (twofold) cover group of
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})

shimura1973modular
, and it usually denoted as
\text{Mp}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
in the literature. For notation simplicity, we shall denote
\text{Mp}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
as
\widetilde{\Gamma}
in the following. It is well-known that can be realized as the group of pairs
(\gamma,\epsilon)
, where
\epsilon=\pm 1
stands for the branch of the square root function. Consequently the group element of
\widetilde{\Gamma}
can be written in the form
shimura1973modular
;
Duncan:2018wbw
;
Kikuchi:2020frp
:

\widetilde{\Gamma}\equiv\left\{[\gamma,\epsilon]\bigl|\gamma\in\Gamma,\ \epsilon\in\{\pm 1\}\right\}\,.

(152)

Obviously each element
\gamma\in\Gamma
corresponds to two elements
\widetilde{\gamma}=\left[\gamma,\pm\right]
of the metaplectic group
\widetilde{\Gamma}
. The multiplication law of arbitrary two elements,
[\gamma_{1},\epsilon_{1}],[\gamma_{2},\epsilon_{2}]\in\widetilde{\Gamma}
, is defined by
14
14

14

Alternative but equivalent definition of
\widetilde{\Gamma}
is given by
shimura1973modular

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Gamma}=\Big\{\widetilde{\gamma}=(\gamma,\epsilon J^{1/2}(\gamma,\tau))~\Big|~\gamma=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma,~~\epsilon=\pm 1\Big\}\,.

The multiplication rule accordingly is

\displaystyle(\gamma_{1},\epsilon_{1}J^{1/2}(\gamma_{1},\tau))(\gamma_{2},\epsilon_{2}J^{1/2}(\gamma_{2},\tau))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2},\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2}J^{1/2}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\tau)J^{1/2}(\gamma_{2},\tau))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2},\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2}\zeta_{1/2}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})J^{1/2}(\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2},\tau))\,,

[\gamma_{1},\epsilon_{1}][\gamma_{2},\epsilon_{2}]=[\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2},\zeta_{1/2}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2}]\,.

(153)

Using the generators
S
and
T
of
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
, the metaplectic group
\widetilde{\Gamma}
can also be generated by two generators
\widetilde{S}
and
\widetilde{T}

bruinier2010weil
;
stromberg2013weil
:

\widetilde{S}=\left[\begin{pmatrix}0~&1\\
-1~&0\end{pmatrix},-1\right],~\quad~\widetilde{T}=\left[\begin{pmatrix}1~&1\\
0~&1\end{pmatrix},+1\right]\,.

(154)

One can check that the generators
\widetilde{S}
and
\widetilde{T}
obey the following relations
Liu:2020msy

\widetilde{S}^{8}=(\widetilde{S}\widetilde{T})^{3}=1\,,

(155)

Consequently
\widetilde{S}
is of order 8, and particularly we have

\widetilde{R}\equiv\widetilde{S}^{2}=\left[\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix},-1\right]\,,~~~\widetilde{S}\widetilde{R}=\widetilde{R}\widetilde{S},~~~\widetilde{T}\widetilde{R}=\widetilde{R}\widetilde{T}\,.

(156)

Consequently the center of
\widetilde{\Gamma}
is the
Z_{4}
subgroup generated by
\widetilde{R}
. Moreover, we have the identities

\widetilde{R}^{2}=\left[\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},-1\right]\,,~~~\widetilde{R}^{2}[\gamma,\epsilon]=[\gamma,-\epsilon]

(157)

Therefore the modular group
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
is isomorphic to the quotient of
\text{Mp}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
over the
Z_{2}
subgroup
Z^{\widetilde{R}^{2}}_{2}=\{1,\widetilde{R}^{2}\}
,

\text{Mp}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})/Z^{\widetilde{R}^{2}}_{2}\cong\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})\,.

(158)

It is known that metaplectic congruence subgroup of level
4N
is
bruinier2010weil
;
stromberg2013weil
:

\widetilde{\Gamma}(4N)=\Big\{\widetilde{h}=\left[h,v(h)\right]~|~h\in\Gamma(4N)\Big\}\,,

(159)

\widetilde{\Gamma}(4N)
is an infinite normal subgroup of
\widetilde{\Gamma}
and it is isomorphic to the principal congruence subgroup
\Gamma(4N)
.
Likewise the finite metaplectic group is the quotient group
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{4N}\equiv\widetilde{\Gamma}/\widetilde{\Gamma}(4N)
. We can check
\widetilde{T}^{4N}
is an element of
\widetilde{\Gamma}(4N)
:

\widetilde{T}^{4N}=\left[\begin{pmatrix}1~&~4N\\
0~&~1\end{pmatrix},~+1\right]\in\widetilde{\Gamma}(4N)\,.

(160)

Hence the generator
\widetilde{T}
of the finite metaplectic group
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{4N}
additionally fulfills

\widetilde{T}^{4N}=1\,.

(161)

For the smallest value
N=1
, the finite metaplectic group
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{4}
denoted as
\widetilde{S}_{4}
is a group of order 96 with group ID
[96,67]
in the computer algebra program
GAP

GAP
. See
Liu:2020msy
for more details about group theory of
\widetilde{S}_{4}
. For larger
N
, the relations in Eqs. (
155
,
161
) are not sufficient and addition relations are needed to render the group
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{4N}
finite. For example, in the case of
N=2
,
the multiplication rules of
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{8}
for the generators
\widetilde{S}
and
\widetilde{T}
are
Liu:2020msy

\widetilde{S}^{2}=\widetilde{R},~~~(\widetilde{S}\widetilde{T})^{3}=\widetilde{R}^{4}=\widetilde{T}^{8}=\widetilde{R}^{2}\widetilde{S}\widetilde{T}^{6}\widetilde{S}\widetilde{T}^{4}\widetilde{S}\widetilde{T}^{2}\widetilde{S}\widetilde{T}^{4}=1,~~~\widetilde{T}\widetilde{R}=\widetilde{R}\widetilde{T}\,.

(162)

Thus
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{8}
is a group of order 768 and its group
ID
is [768,  1085324] in
GAP
.

The weight
k/2
modular forms of
\widetilde{\Gamma}(4N)

span a linear space
\mathcal{M}_{k/2}(\widetilde{\Gamma}(4N))
of finite dimension
k+1
, where
k
is a non-negative integer. In the same manner as shown in the beginning of section
4
, it is always possible to choose the basis of
\mathcal{M}_{k/2}(\widetilde{\Gamma}(4N))
so that the modular forms of weight
k/2
can be arranged into multiplets of the finite metaplectic group
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{4N}

Liu:2020msy
, i.e.

Y_{\mathbf{r}}(\widetilde{\gamma}\tau)=(\epsilon\sqrt{c\tau+d})^{k}\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(\widetilde{\gamma})Y_{\mathbf{r}}(\tau),~~~\widetilde{\gamma}=\left[\gamma,\epsilon\right]\in\widetilde{\Gamma}\,,

(163)

where
\widetilde{\gamma}\tau=\gamma\tau
, and
\rho_{\mathbf{r}}
is an irreducible representation of
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{4N}
. Applying Eq. (
163
) for
\widetilde{\gamma}=\widetilde{R}
, we obtain

Y_{\mathbf{r}}(\widetilde{R}\tau)=Y_{\mathbf{r}}(\tau)=(-i)^{k}\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(\widetilde{R})Y_{\mathbf{r}}(\tau)\,,

(164)

which implies

\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(\widetilde{R})=i^{k},~~~~\begin{cases}k~\texttt{odd}\,~:~\rho^{4}(\widetilde{R})=1\,,\\
k~\texttt{even}:~\rho^{2}(\widetilde{R})=1\,.\end{cases}

(165)

From the dimension formula, we know that there are only two linearly independent weight
1/2
modular forms of level 4 and they can be expressed in terms of the Jacobi theta functions
Novichkov:2020eep
;
Liu:2020msy
:

Y_{1}(\tau)=\theta_{3}(0|2\tau),~~~Y_{2}(\tau)=-\theta_{2}(0|2\tau),

(166)

with

\displaystyle\theta_{2}(0|2\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{2\pi i\tau(m+\frac{1}{2})^{2}}=2q^{1/4}(1+q^{2}+q^{6}+q^{12}+\dots)\,,

\displaystyle\theta_{3}(0|2\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{2\pi i\tau m^{2}}=1+2q+2q^{4}+2q^{9}+2q^{16}+\dots\,,

(167)

It turns out that
Y_{1}(\tau)
and
Y_{2}(\tau)
can be arranged into a doublet
\mathbf{\hat{2}}
of the finite metaplectic group
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{4}\equiv\widetilde{S_{4}}

Liu:2020msy
,

Y^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{\mathbf{\hat{2}}}(\tau)\equiv\begin{pmatrix}Y_{1}(\tau)\\
Y_{2}(\tau)\end{pmatrix}\,,

(168)

which transforms in the two-dimensional irreducible representation
\mathbf{\hat{2}}
of
\widetilde{S_{4}}
,

\displaystyle Y^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{\mathbf{\hat{2}}}(\tau)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S}}{{\longmapsto}}Y^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{\mathbf{\hat{2}}}(-1/\tau)=-\sqrt{-\tau}\,\rho_{\mathbf{\hat{2}}}(\widetilde{S})Y^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{\mathbf{\hat{2}}}(\tau),\,,

\displaystyle Y^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{\mathbf{\hat{2}}}(\tau)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle T}}{{\longmapsto}}Y^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{\mathbf{\hat{2}}}(\tau+1)=\rho_{\mathbf{\hat{2}}}(\widetilde{T})Y^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{\mathbf{\hat{2}}}(\tau)\,,

(169)

where the unitary representation matrices
\rho_{\mathbf{\hat{2}}}(\widetilde{S})
and
\rho_{\mathbf{\hat{2}}}(\widetilde{T})
are given in Ref.
Liu:2020msy
. All half-integer and integer weight modular forms of level 4 can be generated from the tensor products of
Y^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{\mathbf{\hat{2}}}(\tau)
. For example, we find the weight 1 modular forms make up a triplet
\mathbf{\hat{3}^{\prime}}
of
\widetilde{S}_{4}
,

Y_{\mathbf{\hat{3}^{\prime}}}^{(1)}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(Y_{\mathbf{\hat{2}}}^{(\frac{1}{2})}Y_{\mathbf{\hat{2}}}^{(\frac{1}{2})}\right)_{\mathbf{\hat{3}^{\prime}}}=\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{2}\;Y_{1}Y_{2}\\
Y_{1}^{2}\\
-Y_{2}^{2}\\
\end{pmatrix}\,.

(170)

It is straightforward to check that
Y^{(1)}_{\mathbf{\hat{3}^{\prime}}}
is the same as the original weight one modular forms given in
Novichkov:2020eep
up to a permutation, the discrepancy arises from the different convention for the representation matrices of the generators
S
and
T
. In a similar fashion, we can obtain higher weights modular forms and decompose them into different irreducible multiplets of
\widetilde{S}_{4}
, see Ref.
Liu:2020msy
for the details. It is remarkable that
Y_{1}(\tau)
and
Y_{2}(\tau)
are algebraically independent, and each modular form of integral weight
k/2
and level 4 can be written as a polynomial of degree
k+1
in
Y_{1}(\tau)
and
Y_{2}(\tau)
:

\sum_{i=0}^{k}c_{i}Y^{i}_{1}(\tau)Y^{k-i}_{2}(\tau)\,,

(171)

where
c_{i}
are free coefficients. As a result, the construction of higher weight modular forms from
Y_{1}(\tau)
and
Y_{2}(\tau)
naturally bypasses the need to search for non-linear constraints relating redundant higher weight multiplets.

4.5
Multiplier system and explicit form of the rational weight modular forms

Now we turn to the solution to the problem of modular forms for general rational modular weights.
The previous procedure may be followed analogously for the case of modular forms of rational weight
r
for certain congruence subgroups. Similar to Eq. (
150
), a multiplier system
v(\gamma)
is needed such that
v(\gamma)(c\tau+d)^{r}
is the correct automorphy factor satisfying the cocycle relation, and the ambiguity of multi-valued branches caused by the rational power is properly eliminated. For the principal congruence subgroup
\Gamma(N)
of level odd integer
N\geq 5
, a unified construction of multiplier systems denote by
v_{N}(\gamma)
has been given in
ibukiyama2000modular
, and it is found that the corresponding modular forms are of weight
(N-3)/(2N)
. Specifically,
v_{N}
is given by the following formula

v_{N}(\gamma)=\begin{cases}1~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\qquad\text{if}~~c=0\,,\\[7.22743pt]
\exp\left(-2\pi i\dfrac{3\,\text{sign}(c)(N^{2}-1)}{8N}\right)\exp\left(2\pi i\dfrac{N^{2}-1}{8N}\Phi(\gamma)\right)\qquad\text{if}~~c\neq 0\,,\end{cases}

(172)

where
\gamma=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma(N)
, and
\Phi(\gamma)
is given by

\Phi(\gamma)=\begin{cases}\dfrac{b}{d}~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\qquad\qquad\qquad\text{if}~~c=0\,,\\[14.45377pt]
\dfrac{a+d}{c}-12\,\text{sign}(c)\,s(d,|c|)\qquad\text{if}~~c\neq 0\,,\end{cases}

(173)

where
s(d,|c|)
is the Dedekind sum with

s(h,k)=\sum^{k}_{\mu=1}\left(\left(\frac{h\mu}{k}\right)\right)\left(\left(\frac{\mu}{k}\right)\right)\,,

(174)

for integers
h,k(k\neq 0)
.
Here
((x))
is the sawtooth function defined by

((x))=\begin{cases}x-[x]-\frac{1}{2}~\quad~\text{if}~x\notin\mathbb{Z}\,,\\
0~~~~~~~~~~~~~\quad~~\text{if}~x\in\mathbb{Z}\,,\end{cases}

(175)

with
[x]
the floor function. Note that the multiplier system
v_{N}(\gamma)
is an
N
-th root of unity, consequently
v_{N}(\gamma)^{N}=1
for
all
\gamma\in\Gamma(N)
. In short,
v_{N}(\gamma)(c\tau+d)^{(N-3)/2N}
is the automorphy factor for the modular form of weight
(N-3)/(2N)
at level odd integer
N\geq 5
.

Moreover, for any odd integer
5\leq N\leq 13
, the ring of the modular forms of rational weight
r=(N-3)/(2N)
for the principal congruence subgroup
\Gamma(N)
can be constructed from the holomorphic functions
f^{(N)}_{n}(\tau)

ibukiyama2000modular
,

f^{(N)}_{n}(\tau)=\theta_{(\frac{n}{2N},\frac{1}{2})}(N\tau)/\eta(\tau)^{\frac{3}{N}}\,,

(176)

where
n
are odd integers with
1\leq n\leq N-2
, and the theta constant with characteristic
(m^{\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
is defined by

\theta_{(m^{\prime},m^{\prime\prime})}(\tau)=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{2\pi i\tau(\frac{1}{2}(m+m^{\prime})^{2}\tau+(m+m^{\prime})m^{\prime\prime})}\,,

(177)

and Dedekind eta function is

\eta(\tau)=e^{\pi i\tau/12}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1-e^{2\pi in\tau})\,.

(178)

Consequently there are
(N-1)/2
linearly independent modular forms
f^{(N)}_{1},f^{(N)}_{3},\dots f^{(N)}_{N-2}
of rational weight
r=(N-3)/(2N)
, and the graded rings of modular forms
\mathcal{M}(\Gamma(N))=\bigoplus_{m\geq 1}\mathcal{M}_{m\frac{N-3}{2N}}(\Gamma(N))
can be generated by the tensor products of these lowest weight modular forms.
The dimension formula of
\mathcal{M}_{m\frac{N-3}{2N}}(\Gamma(N))
for any odd integer
N\geq 5
and any integer
m>\frac{4(N-6)}{N-3}
is given by
ibukiyama2000modular
;
ibukiyama2020graded

\dim\mathcal{M}_{m\frac{(N-3)}{2N}}(\Gamma(N))=\frac{N^{2}\left[m(N-3)-2(N-6)\right]}{48}\prod_{p|N}(1-\frac{1}{p^{2}})\,,

(179)

where the product is over the prime divisors
p
of
N
. As shown in Eq. (
163
), we expect that rational weight modular forms can be organized into different irreducible multiplets of the finite metaplectic congruence subgroup. We summarize the dimension formula, modular forms of rational weight
r=(N-3)/(2N)
and the corresponding finite metaplectic group in table
6
. The finite metaplectic groups can arise from compactifications on tori with magnetic background fluxes
Kikuchi:2020frp
;
Almumin:2021fbk
. The theory of modular forms with real weight and even complex weight has been developed as well
bruggeman2018holomorphic
, and then the modular group
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
should be extended to the universal covering groups. Some concrete examples are given in
aoki2017jacobi
;
manin2018modular
.

for

, the linear space
\mathcal{M}_{r}(\Gamma(N))|_{k=1}
of the lowest fractional weight modular forms, and the finite metaplectic group
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{N}
. Notice that
\theta_{2,3}(0|2\tau)
and
f^{(N)}_{n}(\tau)
are defined in Eq. (

) and Eq. (

) respectively.

4.6
Most general finite modular group and vector-valued modular form (VVMF)

Let
\rho
denote a
d
-dimensional representation of
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
.
Mathematically this means that each element
\gamma
of
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
is mapped onto its image consisting of one
d\times d
matrix
\rho(\gamma)
.
A vector-valued modular form
Y(\tau)=(Y_{1}(\tau),\dots,Y_{d}(\tau))^{T}
of weight
k
in representation
\rho
is a holomorphic vector-valued function in the upper
half-plane
\mathcal{H}
, and transforms as
knopp2004vector
;
bantay2007vector
;
marks2010structure
;
gannon2014theory
;
franc2016hypergeometric
;
franc2018structure

Y(\gamma\tau)=(c\tau+d)^{k}\rho(\gamma)Y(\tau),~~~\gamma\in\Gamma\,,

(180)

where
k
is called the modular weight and
\rho(\gamma)
is the irreducible representation matrix of
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
. Moreover,
Y(\tau)
is generally required to satisfy the so-called moderate growth condition, i.e., there exists an integer
n
such that for each components
Y_{j}(\tau)
we have
|Y_{j}(\tau)|<\texttt{Im}(\tau)^{n}
for any fixed
\tau
with
\texttt{Im}(\tau)\gg 0
. In other word,
Y_{j}(\tau)
is bounded as
\texttt{Im}(\tau)\rightarrow+\infty
.

In the present work, we will focus on the case that
k
is a non-negative integer and
\rho
has finite image. Finite image means that, although there are infinitely many elements
\gamma
, there are only a finite number of representation matrices
\rho(\gamma)
.
Note that
\rho
can be taken to be unitary since the image of
\rho
is finite, and it is also completely reducible by Maschke’s theorem
rao2006linear
, i.e., it can be decomposed into a direct sum of irreducible representations. Therefore, we can further restrict
\rho
to the unitary irreducible representations without loss of generality. Also the representation matrix
\rho(\gamma)
should have finite order for any
\gamma\in SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
so that one can always work in the basis where
\rho(T)
is diagonal, and the eigenvalues of
\rho(T)
are roots of unity. Finally, the finiteness of the image of
\rho
implies that the kernel
\ker(\rho)
is a normal subgroup of
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
with finite index.

In the original paradigm of modular flavor symmetry
Feruglio:2017spp
, the modular form of level
N
satisfying the condition in Eq. (
100
) is assumed,
\ker(\rho)
is chosen to be the principal subgroup
\Gamma(N)
, and the representation matrices
\rho(\gamma)
form finite modular group
\Gamma_{N}
or
\Gamma^{\prime}_{N}
. In the framework of VVMF,
\Gamma(N)
and the corresponding modular forms are not required, and we start from the more general VVMF obeying Eq. (
180
).

The theory of the VVMF has been developed by Mason et al
marks2009classification
;
marks2010structure
in recent years, and the explicit form of VVMF can be obtained by solving the so-called modular linear differential equation
Liu:2021gwa
;
marks2009classification
;
marks2010structure
. In the following, we report the results for VVMF of low dimensions
Liu:2021gwa
.

•

one-dimensional irreducible VVMFs

It is known that the modular generators
S
and
T
the relations
S^{4}=(ST)^{3}=1
. Consequently the
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
group has 12 one-dimensional irreducible representations denoted as
\mathbf{1}_{p}
with

\mathbf{1}_{p}:~~~~~\rho_{\mathbf{1}_{p}}(S)=i^{p}\,,\qquad\rho_{\mathbf{1}_{p}}(T)=e^{\frac{i\pi}{6}p}\,,

(181)

where
p=0,1,\dots,11
. The lowest weight of the VVMFs in the representation
\mathbf{1}_{p}
is equal to
p
and accordingly the minimal weight VVMF
Y
is found to take the following form

Y=\eta^{2p}\,.

(182)

We would like to mention that
\eta
is the famous Dedekind-eta function defined in Eq. (
118
). One can check that
\eta^{2p}(\tau)
satisfies the following transformation form

\displaystyle\eta^{2p}(S\tau)=(-\tau)^{p}i^{p}\eta^{2p}(\tau)=(-\tau)^{p}\rho_{\mathbf{1}_{p}}(S)\eta^{2p}(\tau),

\displaystyle\eta^{2p}(T\tau)=e^{\frac{i\pi}{6}p}\eta^{2p}(\tau)=\rho_{\mathbf{1}_{p}}(T)\eta^{2p}(\tau)\,.

(183)

Hence
\eta^{2p}(\tau)
is indeed the weight
p
one-dimensional VVMFs in the singlet irrep
\mathbf{1}_{p}
. The free module theorem implies that all one-dimensional VVMFs can be expressed as
marks2010structure
:

\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{1}_{p})=\mathbb{C}[E_{4},E_{6}]\eta^{2p}\,,\qquad p=0,1,\dots,11\,.

(184)

Here
\mathbb{C}[E_{4},E_{6}]
denotes the polynomial of the Eisenstein series
E_{4}(\tau)
and
E_{6}(\tau)

cohen2017modular
:

\displaystyle E_{4}(\tau)=1+240\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sigma_{3}(n)q^{n}=1+240q+2160q^{2}+6720q^{3}+17520q^{4}+\ldots\,,

\displaystyle E_{6}(\tau)=1-504\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sigma_{5}(n)q^{n}=1-504q-16632q^{2}-122976q^{3}-532728q^{4}+\ldots\,,

(185)

where
\sigma_{k}(n)=\sum_{d|n}d^{k}
is the sum of the
k
th power of the divisors of
n
and
q=e^{2\pi i\tau}
. Notice that
E_{4}(\tau)
and
E_{6}(\tau)
are scalar-valued modular forms of
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
with modular weight 4 and 6 respectively.

•

two-dimensional irreducible VVMFs

All the 2-d irreducible unitary representations of
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
with finite image have been fully obtained in Ref.
mason20082
. It is known that there are 54 inequivalent 2-d irreps and up to a possible similar transformation the representation matrices are given by

\displaystyle\rho(T)=\begin{pmatrix}e^{2\pi ir_{1}}&0\\
0&e^{2\pi ir_{2}}\end{pmatrix},\qquad 0\leq r_{1},r_{2}<1\,,

\displaystyle\rho(S)=\frac{(\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2})^{2}}{\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1}}\begin{pmatrix}1&\sqrt{-(\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2})^{5}(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})^{2}-1}\\
\sqrt{-(\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2})^{5}(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})^{2}-1}&-1\end{pmatrix}\,,

(186)

with
\lambda_{1,2}=e^{2\pi ir_{1,2}}
. We will denote the 2-d irreducible representation (irrep) as
\mathbf{2}_{(r_{1},r_{2})}
, and the allowed values of the unordered pair
(r_{1},r_{2})
are given in
Liu:2021gwa
. We can read out the exponent matrix
L
as
L=\texttt{diag}(r_{1},r_{2})
and the minimal weight
k_{0}=6\text{Tr}(L)-1=6(r_{1}+r_{2})-1
. The corresponding minimal weight VVMF in the doublet representation
\mathbf{2}_{(r_{1},r_{2})}
is found to be
Liu:2021gwa
:

Y(\tau)=\begin{pmatrix}~~\eta^{12(r_{1}+r_{2})-2}K^{\frac{6(r_{1}-r_{2})+1}{12}}~{}_{2}F_{1}(\frac{6(r_{1}-r_{2})+1}{12},\frac{6(r_{1}-r_{2})+5}{12};r_{1}-r_{2}+1;K)\\
C\eta^{12(r_{1}+r_{2})-2}K^{\frac{6(r_{2}-r_{1})+1}{12}}~{}_{2}F_{1}(\frac{6(r_{2}-r_{1})+1}{12},\frac{6(r_{2}-r_{1})+5}{12};r_{2}-r_{1}+1;K)\\
\end{pmatrix}

(187)

up to an overall irrelevant constant. The coefficient
C
depends on the explicit form of the representation matrix
\rho(S)
, and its value can be fixed to satisfy the condition Eq. (
180
) of VVMF for
\gamma=S
. The function
K(\tau)
is defined as
K(\tau)=1728/j(\tau)
, where
j(\tau)
is the modular
j
-invariant
cohen2017modular
with the
q
-expansion:

j(\tau)=q^{-1}+744+196884q+21493760q^{2}+\dots\,.

(188)

{}_{n}F_{n-1}
is the generalized hypergeometric series and it is defined by the formula

{}_{n}F_{n-1}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n};b_{1},\dots,b_{n-1};z)=\sum_{m\geq 0}^{\infty}\dfrac{\prod_{j=1}^{n}(a_{j})_{m}}{\prod_{k=1}^{n-1}(b_{k})_{m}}\dfrac{z^{m}}{m!}\,,

(189)

and
(a)_{m}
is the Pochhammer symbol defined by

(a)_{m}=\begin{cases}1\,,~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\quad\qquad m=0\,,\\
a(a+1)\dots(a+m-1)\,,\ \qquad m\geq 1\,.\end{cases}

(190)

Hence we know the module of two-dimensional VVMFs is given by

\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{2}_{(r_{1},r_{2})})=\mathbb{C}[E_{4},E_{6}]Y\oplus\mathbb{C}[E_{4},E_{6}]D_{k_{0}}Y\,,

(191)

where
D_{k_{0}}
is the modular differential operators acting on the VVMFs of weight
k_{0}

Bruinier2008The
,

D_{k_{0}}\equiv\theta-\frac{k_{0}E_{2}}{12}\,,\quad\theta\equiv q\frac{d}{dq}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{d}{d\tau},\quad k_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}\,,

(192)

with
E_{2}(\tau)
being the well-known quasi-modular Eisenstein series of weight 2
cohen2017modular

E_{2}(\tau)=1-24\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sigma_{1}(n)q^{n}=1-24q-72q^{2}-96q^{3}-168q^{4}-144q^{5}+\ldots\,.

(193)

For example, the group
\Gamma_{2}\cong S_{3}
has doublet irreducible representation with
Kobayashi:2018vbk

\rho(S)=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}-1~&~-\sqrt{3}\\
-\sqrt{3}~&~1\end{pmatrix},~~~~\rho(T)=\begin{pmatrix}1~&~0\\
0~&~-1\end{pmatrix}\,,

(194)

which implies
r_{1}=0
and
r_{2}=1/2
and the weight
k_{0}=6(r_{1}+r_{2})-1=2
. From the general expression of two-dimensional VVMF in Eq. (
187
) and considering the
S
transformation, we can fix the parameter
C=\frac{1}{3}
and

Y_{\mathbf{2}_{(0,1/2)}}(\tau)=2\sqrt{3}\begin{pmatrix}~~\eta^{4}K^{-1/6}~{}_{2}F_{1}(-\frac{1}{6},\frac{1}{6};\frac{1}{2};K)\\
\frac{1}{3}\eta^{4}K^{1/3}~{}_{2}F_{1}(\frac{1}{3},\frac{2}{3};\frac{3}{2};K)\\
\end{pmatrix}\,,

(195)

where an overall constant
2\sqrt{3}
is multiplied. The
q
-expansion of
Y_{\mathbf{2}_{(0,1/2)}}(\tau)
is found to be

Y_{\mathbf{2}_{(0,1/2)}}(\tau)=\begin{pmatrix}1+24q+24q^{2}+96q^{3}+24q^{4}+144q^{5}+\ldots\\
8\sqrt{3}\,q^{1/2}\left(1+4q+6q^{2}+8q^{3}+13q^{4}+12q^{5}+\ldots\right)\end{pmatrix}\,.

(196)

It agrees, up to an overall factor, with the
q
-expansion derived in
Kobayashi:2018vbk
, where the

Y_{(0,1/2)}(\tau)
is the weight 2 modular form of level 2 and it is expressed in terms of the eta-function and its derivative.

•

three-dimensional irreducible VVMFs

Similar to the 2-d case, the 3-d irreps of
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
are determined by the three rational numbers
r_{1},r_{2}
and
r_{3}

tuba2001representations
. In the
T
diagonal basis, the 3-d representation matrix
\rho(T)
can be parameterized as

\rho(T)=\begin{pmatrix}e^{2\pi ir_{1}}~&0~&0\\
0~&e^{2\pi ir_{2}}~&0\\
0~&0~&e^{2\pi ir_{3}}\end{pmatrix}\,,\qquad 0\leq r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}<1\,,

(197)

where the parameters
r_{1,2,3}
have to fulfill the following constraint:

r_{1}+r_{2}+r_{3}\in\frac{1}{12}\mathbb{Z}\,.

(198)

This 3-d irrep is denoted as
\mathbf{3}_{(r_{1},r_{2},r_{3})}
. The exponential matrix is
L=\texttt{diag}(r_{1},r_{2},r_{3})
and the minimal weight
k_{0}=4\text{Tr}(L)-2=4(r_{1}+r_{2}+r_{3})-2
. The minimal weight VVMF
Y
in the triplet representation
\mathbf{3}_{(r_{1},r_{2},r_{3})}
is determined to be
Liu:2021gwa

Y(\tau)=\begin{pmatrix}~~\eta^{8(r_{1}+r_{2}+r_{3})-4}K^{\frac{a_{1}+1}{6}}~{}_{3}F_{2}(\frac{a_{1}+1}{6},\frac{a_{1}+3}{6},\frac{a_{1}+5}{6};r_{1}-r_{2}+1,r_{1}-r_{3}+1;K)\\
C_{1}\eta^{8(r_{1}+r_{2}+r_{3})-4}K^{\frac{a_{2}+1}{6}}~{}_{3}F_{2}(\frac{a_{2}+1}{6},\frac{a_{2}+3}{6},\frac{a_{2}+5}{6};r_{2}-r_{3}+1,r_{2}-r_{1}+1;K)\\
C_{2}\eta^{8(r_{1}+r_{2}+r_{3})-4}K^{\frac{a_{3}+1}{6}}~{}_{3}F_{2}(\frac{a_{3}+1}{6},\frac{a_{3}+3}{6},\frac{a_{3}+5}{6};r_{3}-r_{1}+1,r_{3}-r_{2}+1;K)\end{pmatrix}\,,

(199)

with
a_{1}=4r_{1}-2r_{2}-2r_{3},a_{2}=4r_{2}-2r_{1}-2r_{3}
and
a_{3}=4r_{3}-2r_{1}-2r_{2}
. The constants
C_{1,2}
are fixed by the representation matrix of
\rho(S)
in the
T
-diagonal basis. Hence the 3-d VVMFs module is of the form

\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{3}_{(r_{1},r_{2},r_{3})})=\mathbb{C}[E_{4},E_{6}]Y\oplus\mathbb{C}[E_{4},E_{6}]D_{k_{0}}Y\oplus\mathbb{C}[E_{4},E_{6}]D^{2}_{k_{0}}Y\,.

(200)

It is known that there are three linearly independent weight 2 modular forms of level 3 and they can be arranged into a
A_{4}
triplet with the following representation matrices for the modular generators,

\displaystyle\rho(S)=\frac{1}{3}\begin{pmatrix}-1~&~2~&~2\\
2~&~-1~&~2\\
2~&~2~&~-1\end{pmatrix},~~~~\rho(T)=\begin{pmatrix}1~&~0~&~0\\
0~&~\omega~&~0\\
0~&~0~&~\omega^{2}\end{pmatrix},~~~\omega=e^{2\pi i/3}\,,

(201)

which is given by Eq. (
C.2
). This implies
r_{1}=0
,
r_{2}=\frac{1}{3}
and
r_{3}=\frac{2}{3}
and the modular weight
k_{0}=4(r_{1}+r_{2}+r_{3})-2=2
. Taking into account the modular transformation
S
, we find
C_{1}=-\frac{1}{2}
,
C_{2}=-\frac{1}{8}
, and the VVMF in the triplet representation of
A_{4}
is given by

Y_{\mathbf{3}_{(0,1/3,2/3)}}(\tau)=2\sqrt{3}\begin{pmatrix}~~\eta^{4}K^{-1/6}~{}_{3}F_{2}(-\frac{1}{6},\frac{1}{6},\frac{1}{2};\frac{2}{3},\frac{1}{3};K)\\
-\frac{1}{2}\eta^{4}K^{1/6}~{}_{3}F_{2}(\frac{1}{6},\frac{1}{2},\frac{5}{6};\frac{2}{3},\frac{4}{3};K)\\
-\frac{1}{8}\eta^{4}K^{1/2}~{}_{3}F_{2}(\frac{1}{2},\frac{5}{6},\frac{7}{6};\frac{5}{3},\frac{4}{3};K)\end{pmatrix}\,,

(202)

where the overall factor
2\sqrt{3}
is included. We find that the
q
-expansion of
Y_{\mathbf{3}_{(0,1/3,2/3)}}(\tau)
reads as

\displaystyle Y_{\mathbf{3}_{(0,1/3,2/3)}}(\tau)=\begin{pmatrix}1+12q+36q^{2}+12q^{3}+84q^{4}+72q^{5}+\ldots\\
-6q^{1/3}\left(1+7q+8q^{2}+18q^{3}+14q^{4}+31q^{5}+\ldots\right)\\
-18q^{2/3}\left(1+2q+5q^{2}+4q^{3}+8q^{4}+6q^{5}+\ldots\right)\end{pmatrix}\,,

(203)

which exactly coincides with the
q
-expansion derived in
Feruglio:2017spp
, where
Y_{\mathbf{3}_{(0,1/3,2/3)}}(\tau)
is expressed in terms of the eta-function and its derivative. We see that the theory of VVMF provides a convenient way to obtain the explicit expression of modular forms which is the crucial element of modular invariant model.

Finally we summarise some more general finite modular groups.
Since the irrep
\rho
of

\Gamma=SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
has finite image, the image
\text{Im}(\rho)
forms a discrete finite group and it can play the role of discrete flavor symmetry. Due to a
fundamental theorem of homomorphism we can define

\text{Im}(\rho)
as the quotient group

\text{Im}(\rho)\cong\Gamma/\ker(\rho)

where
\Gamma=SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
and

\ker(\rho)
is a normal subgroup determined by the representation
\rho
.
The
\ker(\rho)
consists of the inverse image of the identity elements.

In the paradigm of modular flavor symmetry
Feruglio:2017spp
,
\ker(\rho)
was only restricted to principle congruence subgroups
\Gamma(N)
. The image
\text{Im}(\rho)
is the homogeneous or inhomogeneous finite modular group
\Gamma^{\prime}_{N}=\Gamma/\Gamma(N)
or
\Gamma_{N}=\Gamma/\pm\Gamma(N)
and it is taken as the flavor symmetry to address the flavor structure of quarks and leptons. From the view of VVMF, the kernel
\ker(\rho)
can be a general normal subgroup of
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
instead of
\Gamma(N)
, and accordingly the finite modular groups are not necessarily
\Gamma_{N}
or
\Gamma^{\prime}_{N}
. Thus the framework of modular invariance can be extended significantly by considering VVMFs, and we have more possible choices for the finite modular groups to construct modular invariant models.

In table
7
we list the normal subgroups of
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
with index
\leq 72
as well as the corresponding finite modular group, where we omit the few cases for which the finite modular group is abelian. Besides the known principal congruence subgroups
\Gamma(N)
as well as the inhomogeneous and homogeneous finite modular groups, we see other normal subgroups of
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
. Additional relators are elements of
\ker(\rho)
, when they are added to the
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
presentation relations
S^{4}=(ST)^{3}=1
,
S^{2}T=TS^{2}
, the finite modular groups
\text{Im}(\rho)\cong\Gamma/\ker(\rho)
are produced. Moreover, if the element
S^{2}\in\ker(\rho)
, we have
\rho(S^{2})=\mathds{1}_{d}
. Then the corresponding finite modular group has only even representations, and the VVMFs in these irreps must be of even weights.

It is known that the number of VVMFs in a given irrep
\rho_{Y}
generally increases with the modular weight
k_{Y}
. However, the module
\mathcal{M}(\rho_{Y})
is finitely generated by
d=\dim\rho_{Y}
independent bases denoted by
\{Y_{1},\dots,Y_{d}\}
, thus
Y_{I_{1}...I_{n}}(\tau)
can be written uniformly in the form of
Y^{(k_{Y})}_{\rho_{Y}}=\alpha_{1}Y_{1}+\dots+\alpha_{d}Y_{d}
with
\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{C}[E_{4},E_{6}]
, note that
\alpha_{i}
are polynomials of
E_{4}
and
E_{6}
. Some
\alpha_{i}
for vanishing for small value of modular weight
k_{Y}
, nevertheless there are at most
d
independent alignments
Y_{1}
,
Y_{2}
,…,
Y_{d}
. This implies that the superpotential is strongly constrained by the modular symmetry, and it can only take a finite number of possible forms for a given
\mathcal{G}_{f}
, although there are infinite possible weight and representation assignments for the matter fields.

and the corresponding finite modular groups. It is defined

. Note that
2D_{3}

S_{4}
of type “

”. Additional relators are some elements of

are imposed.

5
CP symmetry and modular invariance

The interplay between the generalized CP symmetry (gCP) and modular symmetry is nontrivial. It is found that the CP transformation can be consistently defined in modular invariant supersymmetric theories with a single modulus
Baur:2019kwi
;
Baur:2019iai
;
Novichkov:2019sqv
. In particular, the modular symmetry can constrain the CP transformation laws of the modulus
\tau
, of chiral matter multiplets and of modular forms
Novichkov:2019sqv
, and several modular invariant models
where CP is spontaneously broken have been constructed
Novichkov:2019sqv
;
Kobayashi:2019uyt
;
Novichkov:2020eep
;
Liu:2020akv
;
Yao:2020zml
;
Yao:2020qyy
;
Okada:2020brs
.
Analogously for multidimensional moduli space, consistent CP definitions have been examined in the context of symplectic modular invariant theories, where the relevant flavour group is the Siegel modular group
Ishiguro:2020nuf
;
Baur:2020yjl
;
Ding:2021iqp
. The CP-conserving vacua in Calabi-Yau compactifications are discussed in Ref.
Ishiguro:2020nuf
.
In this section we formulate the conditions for generalised CP-symmetry consistent with modular invariance, and derive the modular transformations of matter fields and modular forms, before discussing CP fixed points and their implications for flavor theories.

5.1
Generalized CP symmetry compatible with modular invariance

In a theory invariant under
\Gamma=SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
, consistent CP transformations correspond to outer automorphism
u(\gamma)
of
\Gamma

Holthausen:2012dk
;
Chen:2014tpa
:

\mathcal{CP}~\gamma~\mathcal{CP}^{-1}=u(\gamma)~~~.

(204)

Each outer automorphism
u(\gamma)
of
\Gamma
can be described by
Reiner
:

u(\gamma)=\chi(\gamma)~U~\gamma~U^{-1}\,,~~~~\text{or}~~~~u(\gamma)=\chi(\gamma)\gamma\,,

(205)

with

U=\begin{pmatrix}1~&~0\\
0~&~-1\end{pmatrix}

(206)

and the map
\chi(\gamma)
, called character of the modular group, is a homomorphism of
\Gamma
into
\{+1,-1\}
. From the relations
S^{4}=(ST)^{3}=1
satisfied by the modular generators
S
and
T
, we see the modular group has a trivial character with

\chi_{1}(S)=\chi_{1}(T)=1

(207)

and another nontrivial character with

\chi_{2}(S)=\chi_{2}(T)=-1\,.

(208)

Hence the modular group
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
has two independent outer automorphisms
u_{1,2}=\chi_{1,2}(\gamma)~U~\gamma~U^{-1}
, satisfying
the following relations:

u^{2}_{1}=u^{2}_{2}=(u_{1}u_{2})^{2}=\mathds{1}\,.

(209)

Hence the outer automorphism group is isomorphic to a Klein group
K_{4}\cong Z_{2}\times Z_{2}=\{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}=u_{1}u_{2},u_{4}=\mathds{1}\}
15
15

15

We denote by
\mathds{1}
the identity element of the outer automorphism.
. We can derive the action of the outer automorphisms on the modular generators
S
and
T
as follows
Ding:2021iqp
:

\displaystyle~u_{1}(S)=S^{-1}\,,\qquad u_{1}(T)=T^{-1}\,,

\displaystyle~u_{2}(S)=-S^{-1}\,,\qquad u_{2}(T)=-T^{-1}\,,

\displaystyle~u_{3}(S)=-S\,,\qquad u_{3}(T)=-T\,,

\displaystyle~u_{4}(S)=S\,,\qquad u_{4}(T)=T\,.

(210)

We assume that the CP transformation of modulus
\tau
is linear and, for convenience, we write:

\tau\xrightarrow{{\cal CP}}({\cal CP})\tau\equiv\tau_{{\cal CP}}=p_{1}~\tau^{*}+p_{2}\,,

(211)

where
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are parameters to be determined. The inverse CP transformation reads:

\displaystyle\tau^{*}\xrightarrow{{\cal CP}^{-1}}\tau^{*}_{{\cal CP}^{-1}}=\frac{\tau-p_{2}}{p_{1}}\,.

(212)

Enforcing the “consistency condition chain” of Eq. (
204
) on the modular generators
S
and
T
for the automorphisms

u_{a}(\gamma)(a=1,...,4)
, we can obtain:

\displaystyle\frac{p_{2}\tau-p^{2}_{1}-p^{2}_{2}}{\tau-p_{2}}=u_{a}(S)\tau\,,

\displaystyle\tau+p_{1}=u_{a}(T)\tau\,.

(213)

The elements
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
have the same action on
\tau
and similarly for
u_{3,4}
. The above equation can be straightforwardly solved to obtain

p_{1}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}-1,&~\text{for $u_{1,2}$}\\
+1,&~\text{for $u_{3,4}$}\end{array}\right.\,,\qquad~~p_{2}=0\,.

(214)

Thus we have
\tau_{{\cal CP}}=-\tau^{*}
for the automorphisms
u_{1,2}
and
\tau_{{\cal CP}}=\tau^{*}
for
u_{3,4}
. Since
{\tt Im}(\tau_{{\cal CP}})>0
, the admissible CP transformation of the modulus
\tau
is
Baur:2019kwi
;
Baur:2019iai
;
Novichkov:2019sqv
;
Acharya:1995ag
;
Dent:2001cc
;
Giedt:2002ns

\tau\xrightarrow{{\cal CP}}\tau_{{\cal CP}}=-\tau^{*}~\,.

(215)

This represents the correct transformation law of the modulus for both
u_{1,2}
outer automorphisms and we should instead discard
u_{3,4}
. Notice that the same gCP transformation of
\tau
can be derived from the consistency condition, as shown in the Appendix
B
. From Eq. (
215
), we see
\tau^{*}_{\mathcal{CP}^{-1}}=-\tau
and the action of
{\cal CP}
on the modulus is involutive with
{\cal CP}^{2}\tau=\tau
.

Now let us check explicitly that the gCP transformation of Eq. (
215
) is indeed compatible with Eq. (
204
). Applying the consistency transformation chain
\mathcal{CP}\rightarrow\gamma\rightarrow\mathcal{CP}^{-1}
for the modulus
\tau
, we have

\tau\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{CP}}}{{\longrightarrow}}-\tau^{\ast}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\gamma}}{{\longrightarrow}}-\frac{a\tau^{\ast}+b}{c\tau^{\ast}+d}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{CP}^{-1}}=\frac{a\tau-b}{-c\tau+d}=\gamma^{\prime}\tau\,.

(216)

Since
\gamma
and
-\gamma
give the same action on modulus
\tau
, we see that the generalized CP transformation indeed corresponds to the automorphism
u_{1}
or
u_{2}
of the modular group with

u_{1,2}:\gamma\rightarrow\gamma^{\prime}=u_{1,2}(\gamma)=\chi_{1,2}(\gamma)~U~\gamma~U^{-1}=\chi_{1,2}(\gamma)\begin{pmatrix}a~&-b\\
-c~&d\end{pmatrix},~~~\gamma=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma\,,

(217)

where
\chi_{1}(\gamma)
is the trivial character given in Eq. (
207
) and
\chi_{2}(\gamma)
is the nontrivial character in Eq. (
208
).

If we perform a modular transformation
\gamma
and then the CP transformation of Eq. (
215
), we get

\tau\xrightarrow{{\gamma}}\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\xrightarrow{{\cal CP}}(\gamma\circ{\cal CP})\tau=\frac{-a\tau^{*}+b}{-c\tau^{*}+d}\,,

(218)

which is another allowed CP transformation. Since the theory is invariant under
\Gamma
, this choice should not be view as independent from (
215
), which we take as representative element
in the class (
215
) with
a=d=1
,
b=c=0
. We can rewrite Eq. (
218
) as

\tau\xrightarrow{{\gamma\circ{\cal CP}}}\frac{a^{\prime}\tau^{*}+b^{\prime}}{c^{\prime}\tau^{*}+d^{\prime}}~

(219)

with

a^{\prime}=-a,~~~b^{\prime}=b,~~~c^{\prime}=-c,~~~d^{\prime}=d\,.

(220)

One can check

a^{\prime}d^{\prime}-b^{\prime}c^{\prime}=-ad+bc=-(ad-bc)=-1\,.

(221)

Note that the gCP transformation on
\tau
always corresponds to minus determinant with
a^{\prime}d^{\prime}-b^{\prime}c^{\prime}=-1
.

By combining CP and the modular transformations we get the extended modular group
\Gamma^{*}=GL(2,\mathbb{Z})
and the full symmetry transformation of the complex modulus is
Novichkov:2019sqv

\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma^{*}:\quad\begin{cases}\tau\to\cfrac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}&\quad\text{if}\quad ad-bc=1\,,\\
\tau\to\cfrac{a\tau^{*}+b}{c\tau^{*}+d}&\quad\text{if}\quad ad-bc=-1\,.\end{cases}

(222)

where
\gamma=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}
.
The first line of (
222
) refers to a modular transformation, while the second line refers to a CP transformation. For the case of the CP transformation in (
215
) the matrix
\gamma
is represented by
U
in Eq. (
206
). Notice that the action of
\gamma
and
-\gamma
on
\tau
is the same and the full symmetry group acting on moduli is isomorphic to
PGL(2,\mathbb{Z})\equiv GL(2,\mathbb{Z})/\{\pm\mathds{1}_{2}\}
. Here
PGL(2,\mathbb{Z})
is the group of integral
2\times 2
matrices with determinant
\pm 1
, with the matrices
M
and
-M
being identified.

5.2
CP transformations of matter chiral multiplets
\varphi

For a generic chiral supermultiplets
\varphi
, its transfromation properties are characterized by
-k_{\varphi}
and
\rho_{\mathbf{r}}
, where
-k_{\varphi}
is the modular weight of
\varphi
and
\rho_{\mathbf{r}}
is an irreducible representation of the finite modular group
\Gamma_{N}
or
\Gamma^{\prime}_{N}
. Under a modular transformation
\gamma
, the multiplet
\varphi(x)
transforms according to

\varphi(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\gamma}}{{\longrightarrow}}(c\tau+d)^{-k_{\varphi}}\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(\gamma)\varphi(x)\,,~~~\gamma=\begin{pmatrix}a~&b\\
c~&d\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma\,.

(223)

The action of generalized CP symmetry on the multiplet
\varphi
is

\varphi(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{CP}}}{{\longrightarrow}}X_{\mathbf{r}}\overline{\varphi}(x_{\mathcal{P}})\,,

(224)

where
x_{\mathcal{P}}=(t,-\vec{x})
, a bar denotes the hermitian conjugate superfield, and
X_{\mathbf{r}}
is a unitary matrix that acts on flavor space. If we perform a CP transformation, followed by a modular transformation and subsequently an inverse CP transformation, we end up with

\varphi(x)\xrightarrow{{\cal CP}}X_{\mathbf{r}}\overline{\varphi}(x_{\mathcal{P}})\xrightarrow{\gamma}X_{\mathbf{r}}(c\tau^{*}+d)^{-k_{\varphi}}\rho^{*}_{\mathbf{r}}(\gamma)\overline{\varphi}(x_{\mathcal{P}})\xrightarrow{{\cal CP}^{-1}}(c\tau^{*}_{\mathcal{CP}^{-1}}+d)^{-k_{\varphi}}X_{\mathbf{r}}\rho^{*}_{\mathbf{r}}(\gamma)X_{\mathbf{r}}^{-1}\varphi(x)\,,

(225)

where
\tau_{\mathcal{CP}^{-1}}
is the result of applying the inverse CP transformation
\mathcal{CP}^{-1}
to the modulus
\tau
. Since the theory is assumed to be invariant under both the modular and CP transformations, the resulting transformation should be amount to another modular transformation
\gamma^{\prime}
, i.e.,

(c\tau^{*}_{\mathcal{CP}^{-1}}+d)^{-k_{\varphi}}X_{\mathbf{r}}\rho^{*}_{\mathbf{r}}(\gamma)X_{\mathbf{r}}^{-1}\varphi(x)=(c_{\gamma^{\prime}}\tau+d_{\gamma^{\prime}})^{-k_{\varphi}}\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(\gamma^{\prime})\varphi(x)

(226)

which implies

X_{\mathbf{r}}\rho^{*}_{\mathbf{r}}(\gamma)X_{\mathbf{r}}^{-1}=\left(\frac{c_{\gamma^{\prime}}\tau+d_{\gamma^{\prime}}}{c\tau^{*}_{\mathcal{CP}^{-1}}+d}\right)^{-k_{\varphi}}\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(\gamma^{\prime})\,,

(227)

where we have denoted

\gamma^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{\gamma^{\prime}}&b_{\gamma^{\prime}}\\
c_{\gamma^{\prime}}&d_{\gamma^{\prime}}\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma\,.

(228)

From the transformation of the modulus
\tau
in Eq. (
216
), we know the element
\gamma^{\prime}=u_{1,2}(\gamma)
given in Eq. (
217
), thus we can read out

c_{\gamma^{\prime}}=-\chi_{1,2}(\gamma)c,~~~d_{\gamma^{\prime}}=\chi_{1,2}(\gamma)d\,.

(229)

Considering
\tau^{*}_{\mathcal{CP}^{-1}}=-\tau
, the consistency condition of Eq. (
227
) for the modular symmetry and generalized CP symmetry becomes

X_{\mathbf{r}}\rho^{*}_{\mathbf{r}}(\gamma)X^{-1}_{\mathbf{r}}=\chi_{1,2}^{-k_{\varphi}}(\gamma)\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(u_{1,2}(\gamma))\,.

(230)

Since the modular group has two possible characters
\chi_{1,2}(\gamma)
given in Eqs. (
207
,
208
), two kinds of generalized CP transformations could be consistently defined in the context of
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
. The coressponding consistency conditions are given by
Novichkov:2019sqv
;
Ding:2021iqp

X_{\mathbf{r}}\rho^{*}_{\mathbf{r}}(S)X^{-1}_{\mathbf{r}}=\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(S^{-1}),~~~X_{\mathbf{r}}\rho^{*}_{\mathbf{r}}(T)X^{-1}_{\mathbf{r}}=\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(T^{-1})\,.

(231)

for the gCP transformation corresponding to the automorphism
u_{1}
with trivial character
\chi_{1}
in Eq. (
210
). A second gCP transfromation corresponding to
u_{2}
with nontrivial character
\chi_{2}
could be defined
16
16

16

The consistency condition for the second gCP transformation reads as:

X_{\mathbf{r}}\rho^{*}_{\mathbf{r}}(S)X^{-1}_{\mathbf{r}}=\sigma\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(S^{-1}),~~~X_{\mathbf{r}}\rho^{*}_{\mathbf{r}}(S)X^{-1}_{\mathbf{r}}=\sigma\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(T^{-1}),~~~\sigma=(-1)^{-k_{\varphi}}\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(S^{2})\,.

Obviously the second gCP transformation
X_{\mathbf{r}}
depends on
\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(S^{2})
and the modualr weight
k_{\varphi}
, and it can be defined if and only if the level
N
is even, the dimension of representation
\rho_{\mathbf{r}}
is even, and the traces of
\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(T)
and
\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(S)
are vanishing
Novichkov:2020eep
.
, nevertheless it is difficult to build phenomenologically viable models of fermion masses and mixing exploiting this second gCP transformation
Novichkov:2020eep
. Hence we shall be concerned with the gCP transformation defermined by Eq. (
231
) in this work.

In the basis where both
\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(S)
and
\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(T)
are symemtric matrices, one has
\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(S^{-1})=\rho^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{r}}(S)=\rho^{*}_{\mathbf{r}}(S)
and
\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(T^{-1})=\rho^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{r}}(T)=\rho^{*}_{\mathbf{r}}(T)
so that one can determine
X_{\mathbf{r}}=\mathds{1}_{\mathbf{r}}
up to an irrelevant phase, i.e., the gCP transformation has the canonical form. If all the Clebsch-Gordon coeffcients of the finite modular group are real in the above symemtric basis, the gCP symemtry would enforce all coupling constants in the Lagrangian to be real. Thus the real part of modulus
\tau
would be the unique source of CP violation, and gCP symemtry can enhance the predictive power of modular invariant models. For the finite modular groups
\Gamma_{N}
and
\Gamma^{\prime}_{N}
with level
N\leq 6
, it is known that the symmetric basis can always be achieved.

5.3
CP transformations of modular form
Y_{\mathbf{r}}(\tau)

Considering a multiplet
Y_{\mathbf{r}}(\tau)
of modular forms of level
N
and weight
k_{Y}
, it transforms in certain irreducible representation
\rho_{\mathbf{r}}
of the finite modular group
\Gamma_{N}
or
\Gamma^{\prime}_{N}
, i.e.

Y_{\mathbf{r}}(\gamma\tau)=(c\tau+d)^{k_{Y}}\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(\gamma)Y_{\mathbf{r}}(\tau),~~~\gamma\in\Gamma\,.

(232)

In general, there can be several linearly independent such multiplets, particularly for higher weight corresponding large
k_{Y}
. We start by examining the case where there is only one. Under gCP it transforms as:

Y_{\mathbf{r}}(\tau)\xrightarrow{\cal CP}Y_{\mathbf{r}}(-\tau^{*})\,.

(233)

We can prove that the modular multiplet
Y_{\mathbf{r}}(\tau)
has the same gCP transformation as that of matter fields in Eq. (
224
), i.e.,

Y_{\mathbf{r}}(-\tau^{*})=X_{\mathbf{r}}Y_{\mathbf{r}}^{*}(\tau)\,.

(234)

In order to show this, we define

\widetilde{Y}_{\mathbf{r}}(\tau)=X_{\mathbf{r}}^{-1*}Y_{\mathbf{r}}^{*}(-\tau^{*})\,,

(235)

where
X_{\mathbf{r}}
is the solution of the consistency condition Eq. (
231
) for the concerned automorphism
u_{1}
. The modular transfromation of
\widetilde{Y}_{\mathbf{r}}(\tau)
under a generic element
\gamma\in\Gamma
is

\displaystyle\widetilde{Y}_{\mathbf{r}}(\gamma(\tau))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle X_{\mathbf{r}}^{-1*}Y_{\mathbf{r}}^{*}(-(\gamma(\tau))^{*})

(236)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle X_{\mathbf{r}}^{-1*}Y_{\mathbf{r}}^{*}(u_{1}(\gamma)(-\tau^{*}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle X_{\mathbf{r}}^{-1*}(c\tau+d)^{k_{Y}}\rho^{*}_{\mathbf{r}}(u_{1}(\gamma))~Y_{\mathbf{r}}^{*}(-\tau^{*})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(c\tau+d)^{k_{Y}}~\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(\gamma)~\widetilde{Y}_{\mathbf{r}}(\tau)\,,

where the identities
-(\gamma(\tau))^{*}=u(\gamma)(-\tau^{*})
and
X_{\mathbf{r}}\rho^{*}_{\mathbf{r}}(\gamma)X^{-1}_{\mathbf{r}}=\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(u_{1}(\gamma))
have been used
17
17

17

From Eq. (
216
), we know
\mathcal{CP}\circ\gamma\circ\mathcal{CP}^{-1}=u_{1}(\gamma)
which gives
\mathcal{CP}\circ\gamma=u_{1}(\gamma)\circ\mathcal{CP}
. Consequently we have
\mathcal{CP}\circ\gamma(\tau)=u_{1}(\gamma)\circ\mathcal{CP}(\tau)
which leads to
-(\gamma(\tau))^{*}=u_{1}(\gamma)(-\tau^{*})
. Moreover,
From Eq. (
230
), we
know

X_{\mathbf{r}}\rho^{*}_{\mathbf{r}}(\gamma)X^{-1}_{\mathbf{r}}=\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(u_{1}(\gamma))
which gives
\rho^{*}_{\mathbf{r}}(\gamma)X^{-1}_{\mathbf{r}}=X^{-1}_{\mathbf{r}}\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(u_{1}(\gamma))
and
\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(\gamma)X^{-1*}_{\mathbf{r}}=X^{-1*}_{\mathbf{r}}\rho^{*}_{\mathbf{r}}(u_{1}(\gamma))
.
.
Since
\widetilde{Y}_{\mathbf{r}}(\tau)
and
Y_{\mathbf{r}}(\tau)
transform in the same way and, by assumption, there is only one linearly independent such modular form, we conclude that they are proportional,
\widetilde{Y}_{\mathbf{r}}(\tau)\propto Y_{\mathbf{r}}(\tau)

which gives:

Y_{\mathbf{r}}(-\tau^{*})=\lambda~X_{\mathbf{r}}Y^{*}_{\mathbf{r}}(\tau)\,.

(237)

By performing two gCP transformation in succsssion, we obtain

Y_{\mathbf{r}}(\tau)=|\lambda|^{2}~X_{\mathbf{r}}X_{\mathbf{r}}^{*}Y_{\mathbf{r}}(\tau)=|\lambda|^{2}~Y_{\mathbf{r}}(\tau)\,,

(238)

where we have chosen the gCP transfroamtion
X_{\mathbf{r}}
to be a constant unitary symmetric matrix fulfilling
X_{\mathbf{r}}X_{\mathbf{r}}^{*}=1
which corresponds to
\mathcal{CP}^{2}=1
18
18

18

The action of
{\cal CP}
in the modulus, see Eq. (
215
), is involutive, that is
{\cal CP}^{2}\tau=\tau
. The action is
\mathcal{CP}^{2}
on the matter field is
\varphi(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{CP}^{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}X_{\mathbf{r}}X^{*}_{\mathbf{r}}\varphi(x)
. From the consistency condtion in Eq. (
224
), we obtain:

(c\tau+d)^{-k_{\varphi}}X_{\mathbf{r}}X_{\mathbf{r}}^{*}~\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(\gamma)~X^{-1*}_{\mathbf{r}}X_{\mathbf{r}}^{-1}\varphi=(c\tau+d)^{-k_{\varphi}}\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(\gamma)\varphi~~~.

By the Schur’s Lemma, the product
X_{\mathbf{r}}X_{\mathbf{r}}^{*}
is proportional to the identity. It acts without conjugating the matter fields and represents the element
\gamma_{{\cal CP}^{2}}
or, more precisely, the element of the finite group
\Gamma_{N}
or
\Gamma^{\prime}_{N}
that corresponds to
\gamma_{{\cal CP}^{2}}
. As a consequence we have:

X_{\mathbf{r}}X_{\mathbf{r}}^{*}=\mathds{1}_{\mathbf{r}}~~\text{or}~~X_{\mathbf{r}}X_{\mathbf{r}}^{*}=\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(S^{2})=\pm\mathds{1}_{\mathbf{r}}\,.

Therefore we must have
X_{\mathbf{r}}X_{\mathbf{r}}^{*}=\mathds{1}_{\mathbf{r}}
for the inhomegenous finite modular group
\Gamma_{N}
.
. The non-vanishing constant
\lambda
can be absorbed by an appropriate choice of phase of the whole multiplet
Y_{\mathbf{r}}(\tau)
so that we have

Y_{\mathbf{r}}(\tau)\xrightarrow{\cal CP}Y_{\mathbf{r}}(-\tau^{*})=X_{\mathbf{r}}Y_{\mathbf{r}}^{*}(\tau)\,.

(239)

Hence the modular multiplets have the same gCP transformation law as that of matter fields.

If there are
s
linearly independent multiplets
Y^{a}_{\mathbf{r}}(\tau)

(a=1,\ldots,s)
transforming as in Eq. (
232
), Eq. (
236
) holds individually for all
\widetilde{Y}^{a}_{\mathbf{r}}(\tau)=X_{\mathbf{r}}^{-1*}Y^{a*}_{\mathbf{r}}(-\tau^{*})
and we
have:

Y^{a}_{\mathbf{r}}(-\tau^{*})=\lambda^{a}_{~b}~X_{\mathbf{r}}Y^{b*}_{\mathbf{r}}(\tau)~~~,

(240)

where
\lambda^{a}_{~c}\lambda^{*c}_{~~b}X_{\mathbf{r}}X^{*}_{\mathbf{r}}=\delta^{a}_{~b}\mathds{1}_{\mathbf{r}}
. When
X_{\mathbf{r}}
is involutive with
X_{\mathbf{r}}X^{*}_{\mathbf{r}}=\mathds{1}_{\mathbf{r}}
we have
\lambda^{a}_{~c}\lambda^{*c}_{~~b}=\delta^{a}_{~b}
, it is always possible to factorize the matrix
\lambda
into
\lambda=\eta^{-1}\eta^{*}
and we obtain
19
19

19

If
(\mathds{1}+\lambda)
is invertible, we take
\eta=(\mathds{1}+\lambda)^{-1}
, then
\lambda\eta^{*-1}=\eta^{-1}
.
If
(\mathds{1}+\lambda)
is not invertible, we can always find a complex number
u
with
|u|=1
such that
-u^{2}
is not an eigenvalue of
\lambda
. Hence,
\lambda+u^{2}\mathds{1}
is invertible. In this case, we take
\eta=(u^{-1}\lambda+u\mathds{1})^{-1}
, then
\lambda\eta^{*-1}=\eta^{-1}
. This construction was given by Prof. Marc van Leeuwen
que:matrix_decom
.
:

\eta^{a}_{~b}Y^{b}_{\mathbf{r}}(-\tau^{*})=X_{\mathbf{r}}[\eta^{a}_{~b}Y^{b}_{\mathbf{r}}]^{*}(\tau)~~~.

(241)

We see that, by performing the change of basis
Y^{a}_{\mathbf{r}}(\tau)\to\eta^{a}_{~b}Y^{b}_{\mathbf{r}}(\tau)
, Eq. (
236
) holds independently for each multiplet
Y^{a}(\tau)
. In short, when the action of gCP on the field space is involutive with
X_{\mathbf{r}}X^{*}_{\mathbf{r}}=\mathds{1}_{r}
, one can properly choose the basis of the modular forms such that matter fields and modular forms transform in a similar way under gCP, i.e.,

\varphi(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{CP}}}{{\longrightarrow}}X_{\mathbf{r}}\overline{\varphi}(x_{\mathcal{P}})\,,~~~Y^{a}(\tau)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{CP}}}{{\longrightarrow}}Y^{a}_{\mathbf{r}}(-\tau^{*})=X_{\mathbf{r}}Y^{b*}_{\mathbf{r}}(\tau)\,.

(242)

In this case, it is convenient to move to the basis where
X_{\mathbf{r}}=\mathds{1}_{\mathbf{r}}
20
20

20

The CP transfromation matrix
X_{\mathbf{r}}
would be a unitary and symmetric matrix if
X_{\mathbf{r}}X^{*}_{\mathbf{r}}=\mathds{1}_{\mathbf{r}}
, and the corresponding Takagi factorization is
X_{\mathbf{r}}=\Omega_{\mathbf{r}}\Omega^{T}_{\mathbf{r}}
where
\Omega_{\mathbf{r}}
is a unitary matrix. If we perform a change of basis with a unitary matrix
\Omega_{\mathbf{r}}
in the field space:
\varphi^{\prime}=\Omega^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{r}}\varphi
, it is easy to see that the gCP transformation of
\varphi^{\prime}
is
\varphi^{\prime}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{CP}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\overline{\varphi^{\prime}}(x_{\mathcal{P}})
.
. Then the consistency condition of Eq. (
231
) implies that the representation matrices
\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(S)
and
\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(T)
of the modular generators are symmetric. As we explained early, if both
\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(S)
and
\rho_{\mathbf{r}}(T)
are symmetric and unitary matrices, we would have
X_{\mathbf{r}}=\mathds{1}_{\mathbf{r}}
which is exactly the canonical CP transformation. If the Clebsh-Gordan coefficients are all real in the adopted basis, the requirement of gCP invairance would enforces all free parameters in the superpotential to be real.

5.4
CP fixed points

In a modular invariant theory with gCP symmetry, the vacuum expectation value of the modulus
\tau
would be the unique source breaking modular and gCP symmetry. However, there exist certain values of
\tau
which conserve CP. If
\tau
is left invariant by CP transformation of Eq. (
215
), i.e.

\tau\xrightarrow{{\cal CP}}\tau_{{\cal CP}}=-\tau^{*}=\tau\,.

(243)

We see
\tau
lies on the imaginary axis,
\Re\tau=0
. The most general gCP transfromation of
\tau
is
\gamma\circ{\cal CP}
given by Eq. (
218
), it is the composition of the a generic modular transformation
\gamma
and the CP transformation of Eq. (
215
). Thus the most general value of
\tau
preserving the residual gCP symmetry
\gamma\circ{\cal CP}
satisfies the following condition

(\gamma\circ{\cal CP})\tau=\tau,~~~\gamma=\begin{pmatrix}a~&~b\\
c~&~d\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma\,,

(244)

which gives

\frac{-a\tau^{*}+b}{-c\tau^{*}+d}=\tau\,,

(245)

or equivalently

\displaystyle c\left(\Re\tau\right)^{2}-\left(a+d\right)\Re\tau+b+c\left(\Im\tau\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\,,

\displaystyle\left(a-d\right)\Im\tau

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\,.

(246)

Thus we have

\displaystyle a=d\,,

(247)

and

\displaystyle\Re\tau=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\dfrac{a}{c}\pm\frac{1}{c}\sqrt{1-c^{2}\left(\Im\tau\right)^{2}}\,,&~c\neq 0\\
\dfrac{b}{2a}\,,&~c=0\end{array}\right.

Without loss of generality, we firstly consider that
\tau
lies in the fundamental domain. Solving the constraints in Eqs. (
247
,
5.4
) together with the determinant condition
ad-bc=1
, we find the CP conserved values of
\tau
are the imaginary axis and the boundary of the fundamental domain
\mathcal{D}
, the corresponding residual CP symmetry is summarized in table
8
. At the intersection points, the residual symmetry is enhanced. For instance, the modulus
\tau=e^{2i\pi/3}
is invariant under both CP transformations
T^{-1}\circ\mathcal{CP}
and
S\circ\mathcal{CP}
so that it preserves the modular symmetry
ST
which is generated by the two residual CP transfromations. Thus the full residual symmetry group of
\tau=e^{2i\pi/3}
is
\left(Z^{ST}_{3}\rtimes Z^{S\circ\mathcal{CP}}_{2}\right)\times Z^{R}_{2}
defined as

\displaystyle\left(Z^{ST}_{3}\rtimes Z^{S\circ\mathcal{CP}}_{2}\right)\times Z^{R}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big\{ST,R,S\circ\mathcal{CP}|R^{2}=(S\circ\mathcal{CP})^{2}=(ST)^{3}=1,(ST)R=R(ST),

(251)

\displaystyle\hskip-14.45377pt(S\circ\mathcal{CP})R=R(S\circ\mathcal{CP}),(S\circ\mathcal{CP})ST(S\circ\mathcal{CP})^{-1}=(ST)^{-1}\Big\}\,.

Similarly the symmetric point
\tau=i
is invariant under both modular transfromation
S
and CP transformation
\mathcal{CP}
, and consequently the residual symmetry group is
Z^{S}_{4}\rtimes Z^{\mathcal{CP}}_{2}=\left\{S,\mathcal{CP}|S^{4}=1,\mathcal{CP}^{2}=1,\mathcal{CP}\,S\,\mathcal{CP}^{-1}=S^{-1}\right\}
. Moreover, the symmetric point
\tau=i\infty
is invariant under
T
,
R
and
\mathcal{CP}
, and therefore it preserves the residual symmetry group

\left(Z^{T}\rtimes\mathcal{CP}\right)\times Z^{R}_{2}=\left\{T,R,\mathcal{CP}|R^{2}=\mathcal{CP}^{2}=1,\mathcal{CP}\,T\,\mathcal{CP}^{-1}=T^{-1},\mathcal{CP}\,R=R\,\mathcal{CP},TR=RT\right\}\,.

(252)

Figure 9:
The CP conserving lines in the fundamental domain
\mathcal{D}
are displayed in blue and the corresponding residual CP symmetries are labelled. The intersection points
\tau=e^{\pi i/3},\,i,e^{2\pi i/3}
labelled in red enjoy two different residual CP symmetries so that a residual modular symmetry can be generated. For instance, the self-dual point
\tau=i
is invariant under both CP transfromations
Z^{\mathcal{CP}}_{2}
and
Z^{S\circ\mathcal{CP}}_{2}
, consequently it is also invariant under the modular generator
S
.

We plot the CP conserving points and the corresponding residual CP symmetries in figure
9
. Furthermore we find that all the CP conserving points in the upper half complex plane are related to those in the fundamental domain by some modular transformation, and the corresponding generalized CP stabilizer given by

\tau^{\prime}=\gamma_{1}\tau^{\mathcal{CP}}_{0}\,,~~~~\gamma^{\prime}\circ\mathcal{CP}=\gamma_{1}\gamma^{\mathcal{CP}}_{0}\circ\mathcal{CP}\,\gamma^{-1}_{1}\,,

(253)

where
\gamma_{1}
is an arbitrary modular transformation,
\tau^{\mathcal{CP}}_{0}
and
\gamma^{\mathcal{CP}}_{0}
take the values summarized in table

.

is defined by the condition

which is a special case of Eq. (

) with
\tau^{\mathcal{CP}}_{0}
in the fundamental domain. We would like to mention that the definition of
\gamma\circ\mathcal{CP}
is given in Eq. (

). Notice that the modular generators

and

) and
